Figure 5.10. Application of the algorithm to delineate trees at the plot scale using a UAV image (western part of the largest cherry tree of the study area) [SOURCE: Page_169]

The algorithm was applied to all orchards within the watershed. Validation was done from a random sample of 50 fields. Fig 5.11 shows the correlations obtained with the GSH images (Fig 5.11 a) and the Pleiades images (Fig 5.11b). The correlation coefficients are high for both cases. The largest field with more than 1000 trees impacts the statistics. If this last field is removed, the performances are lower with (r²=0.75 with GSH images, r²=0.72 with Pleaides, see graphs in supplementary material Fig S$_5$.5-S$_5$.6). Some underestimation in the number of trees is observed for several fields when using the GSH images. Analysis of those fields revealed that the orchards were very heterogeneous. The detection worked poorly for a particular apricot orchard, where it estimated only 75 trees, compared to 331 observed trees. This parcel was very heterogeneous, with young and old trees and with a large soil moisture variability visible at the surface (with dark and light areas). Projected shadows and various contrasts in the background likely distorted the analysis and the number of trees counted, explaining the deficient results obtained for this field. The acquisition date of images used for delineation of trees is important to consider. If the canopy is well developed, a problem can also appear with the tree overlap for some fields leading to an underestimation of the number of trees. [SOURCE: Page_169]

Figure 5.11. Validation of the identification of the number of fields with a) GSH images (mean manual counting: 102 trees) and b) Pleiades images (mean manual counting: 108 trees, the date is not the same than GSH) on 50 fields [SOURCE: Page_169]

169 [SOURCE: Page_169]

From these results, the tree density can be assessed for all orchards of the Ouvèze area. The median was in the order of 75 trees/orchard (median value from GSH) and 69 from Pleaides. The median of the tree density was in the order of 267 trees/ha (from GSH) and 242 from Pleaides. As expected, larger plots have greater number of trees. However, we observed a slight difference according to the species as displayed in Fig 5.12. Olive trees are generally less spaced than apricot and cherry trees, with a mean density of 304 trees/ha against 222 for cherry trees and 244 for apricot (according to the results with GSH). [SOURCE: Page_170]

Figure 5.12. Comparison between the number of trees estimated from GSH images and the area of fields for a) cherry trees, b) apricot trees and c) olive trees [SOURCE: Page_170]

The previous assessment of plot variables computed from remote sensing (i.e., number of trees per field) was used to estimate water consumption of orchards according to Eq. 5.2 and 5.3 (described in Section 2.4). Accurate information on the irrigation equipment, flow and irrigation duration from farmers were also needed. This technical information was available from farmer surveys representative of the variability of agricultural practices encountered in the watershed. Values obtained for water consumption varied from 8 to 910 mm with an average in the order of magnitude of 244 mm. For example, at field scale, from field survey with water sensors (see Fig. S$_5$.3a, Table S$_5$.2), a water balance neglecting drainage, indicates 200 to 300 mm of water added as irrigation from May to September depending on the year. At the farm scale, comparisons have been performed between three farms (representative of the diversity of the agricultural practices, two used drip irrigation with different schedules, the third use micro-sprinkler). Fig 5.13 shows the results obtained for the different irrigation water volumes estimated by the three methods (described in eq 5.1 (WR considered as the reference according to regional guidelines (Kc method), eq 5.2 (WF from surveys and remote sensing data) and WASA) for three farmers and three contrasted years (2016: normal, 2017: dry and 2018 classified as wet, see table S$_5$.1 in supplementary material). There are uncertainties at different levels. The ASA data are associated with approximately 30% uncertainty due to network losses and misrepresentations, which is accounted for by including an error bar (blue box). Furthermore, for the calculation of WF, some farmers have given us very accurate information on their irrigation practises and strategies according to the wet or dry years (they generally bring less water for wet year), while others were more inaccurate indicating only a standard practice, without changes according to the year climate. When information was available, error bars were added to irrigation water volumes computed from surveys (WF, green boxes). In addition to uncertainties according to the climatic years, other uncertainties can be due to the other factors occurring in eq 5.3, among them the number of trees par plot. In order to quantify the [SOURCE: Page_170]

170 [SOURCE: Page_170]

impact of this uncertainty on the assessment of water brought by the farmer, we have computed the water dose for one field considering in the first case 75 trees (estimated from GSH images) and 69 trees from Pleiades. The hourly intake when deploying micro-sprinklers (flow at 70 L/h and with 1 micro sprinkler per tree) 5250 litres per hour for GSH and 4830 litres per hour with Pleiades. This difference can be significant at the farm scale having an average of more than twenty irrigated plots. The analysis of the results shows that for the A$_1$ farmer, the three methods do not vary between normal and dry years. For the A$_2$ farmer, values from surveys (WF) are always lower than the two other methods except for the wet year. On the other hand, the B$_1$ farmer consumes more than the two other farms and above the crop needs (expressed in red). Overall, we can identify three clear behaviours: one that is more water-efficient (A$_2$), one that is more water intensive (B$_1$), and the last (A$_1$) which seems to follow recommendations. [SOURCE: Page_171]

Figure 5.13. Comparisons between water requirements (WR), water consumed from ASA (WASA) and water used for irrigation computed from farm surveys and variables derived from remote sensing. [SOURCE: Page_171]

4. Discussion •   Monitoring orchard development and mapping young orchards from Sentinel 2 Although the spatial resolution of Sentinel 2 is 10m, it allowed to identify the young plantations of fruit trees at regional scale. A simplified approach based on a thresholding of NDVI gave accurate maps (98% global accuracy) of young orchards for the 7 studied years. (Nageswara Rao et al. 2004) have shown spectral NDVI profiles of different crops and also observed that young mango (planted less than 5 years) has relatively low profile throughout indicating that the amount of green cover is low. The leaf development and the senescence can be easily detected from Sentinel 2 times series. A typical pattern was observed for orchards, with a first increase from March to June then a plateau more and less [SOURCE: Page_171]

171 [SOURCE: Page_171]

deepened in summer according to the stress and then at the end from October a slight decrease of NDVI. Similar profiles were observed by (Zhu et al. 2020) on apple and cherry trees in China. The effect of water stress is less marked in this study case due to the climate. [SOURCE: Page_172]

•     Water needs and contribution of remote sensing data The simplified FAO method based only on crop coefficient and evapotranspiration presents many computational limits. Among them, the limited attention given to soil characteristics that can have a strong influence on the available water for the crop. Many papers have proposed various approaches using remote sensing to derive Kc, with several including the addition of a stress coefficient to improve this simplified approach (Pereira et al., 2020; Pôças et al., 2020; Simonneaux et al., 2008). However, Pôças et al. (2020), consider that for an improved estimation of crop water requirements, these methods should be used in combination with soil water balance models. The surface characteristics such as grassed or not, together with inter-row management, are then useful parameters that can improve the information about water competition. In this study, we have compared different methods to identify grassed orchards from non-grassed, comparing range of remote sensing platforms. An accuracy of 79% of orchards with a correct identification of the inter- row (grassed or non-grassed) was obtained from Sentinel 2. This accuracy increases with the use of finer resolution images (freely accessible, GSH 81%) but the performances are also highly dependent on the date of image acquisition. Other approaches based on the analysis of times series of Sentinel 2 data have been applied to detect inter-row of vineyards by (Abubakar et al. 2023; Palazzi et al. 2022). Abukabar et al. (2023) have identified a background signal which they subtracted to the Sentinel 2 LAI time series to classify the grassed and non-grassed plots. This method seems difficult to implement to orchards because the background signal presents different spectral signatures (fruit trees are bigger than vineyard plants and the signal can be confused for young plantations. (Ronchetti et al. 2020) have compared five classification algorithms to monitor crop variability from UAV data. If the performances are high for their various studied crops, they showed that for pear orchards, the detection methods were the noisiest, because of the presence of weeds, the effect of shadows depending of the acquisition date and time. Moreover, the use of UAV is limited and often expensive to be applied at larger regional scale. The soil characteristics of orchards are also important to consider. For the moment, only soil maps that have variable accuracy at small scales are available to be used. Some approaches have proposed digital soil map combining remote sensing (Gomez et al., 2019; Lagacherie, 2008), but these methods are difficult to implement for orchards and require large areas of bare soils. [SOURCE: Page_172]

•     Assessment of irrigation volumes The assessment of irrigation water volumes from surveys (WF) requires accurate data on irrigation material and on the irrigation time. It is still difficult to get this information for all the farms of the watershed. The assumption is to consider a statistical representative sample of farm surveys including various practices. More ground surveys must be done to acquire more data to build relationships on the water volume brought to young orchards compared to old plots. While the number of trees can be estimated with satisfactory performance from high resolution [SOURCE: Page_172]

172 [SOURCE: Page_172]

data (GSH, Pleaides), the accuracy depends on the sensor type and on the acquisition image date. Some over- and under-estimation has been observed for young orchards or very heterogenous fields, with misclassification and poor delineation occasionally resulting. Higher accuracy (>90% of correct estimations) are obtained on the number of trees using UAV images at finer spatial resolution on various orchard types by Dong et al. (2020) or Johansen et al. (2018), but UAV can be expensive (time and for data processing) and has a small sampling footprint compared to Pleiades or GSH. The proposed simplified method applied to Pleaides or GSH images makes it possible to estimate the number of trees for all the orchards in a catchment area. Ozdarici-ok and Ok, (2023) have reviewed various techniques and sensors types to identify orchard trees. No cherry trees studies are reported. Most of the references cited used methods based on deep learning algorithms requiring a large dataset for training the networks, or classifications. The acquisition dates of images at high resolution also have an impact on the results, particularly for tree discrimination. According to the homogeneity of the surface (inter-row and tree), the detection method can miss some trees, especially if there is a mixture of young and old trees within the field. The date of acquisition is an important consideration for the retrieval of certain parameters and can influence the quality of detection of trees (i.e. the development of the tree canopy or grass cover is dependent on timing). To improve the robustness of the results, it is recommended to collect some imagery in winter or earlier spring to better identify if there is grass in the inter-rows from Sentinel 2 and other finer resolution platforms. Antecedent conditions can also affect retrieval results. For example, the choice of Pleiades acquisition tasking should avoid periods after heavy rain, since large surface soil heterogeneities can confound the classification of orchard plots. While the use of GSH offers advantages in terms of resolution, there is little control regarding the timing of collection. Despite these constraints, the use of free data that can be easily accessed from various online resources makes the proposed methods more operational and less costly than using UAV or commercial satellite data, or making collecting laborious ground- based observations. Alternative remote sensing methods are also available to be explored to improve the assessment of the number of trees e.g. LIDAR sensors (Dian et al., 2023; Tsoulias et al., 2020). An ambitious program^32 coordinated by IGN (Institut national de l’Information Géographique) aims to map all the France territory by LIDAR at very fine resolution. With these new data, we can expect to have tree level information that is more accurate which can complement existing methods. Likewise, while the use of radar and thermal sensors was not addressed in this study, they also offer directions to be explored to have complementary information of the soil moisture and irrigation practices (Courault et al., 2022; McCabe et al., 2019). For the moment operational satellites which have thermal bands do not have a sufficient spatial or temporal resolution for tracking agricultural practices on small fields (such as those within the Ouvèze watershed). The future TRISHNA missions^33 which should be launched in 2025 (Lagouarde et al., 2019; Rama et al., 2023) should improve these last points. [SOURCE: Page_173]

32 https://www.ign.fr/institut/lidar-hd-vers-une-nouvelle-cartographie-3d-du-territoire) 33 https://earthobservation.magellium.com/project/trishna/ [SOURCE: Page_173]

173 [SOURCE: Page_173]

5. Conclusion Here we evaluated a range of approaches to characterize agroinformatic variables typical of Mediterranean orchards using a variety of remote-sensing based platforms. With increasing drought periods due to climate changes and water restrictions occurring in many regions, it is crucial to have an accurate assessment of the real water requirements at the river basin scale to improve water resource management. Sentinel 2 images with high spatial and temporal resolution allow for the monitoring of leaf development and inter-row management with satisfactory results. A simple thresholding applied on the NDVI temporal profiles computed for each orchard allowed for the classification of young from old plantations, which require different irrigation strategies, reasonably well. The use of images with finer spatial resolutions such as Pleaides data or GSH improved the assessment of grassed from non-grassed orchards and allowed the quantification of the number of trees per field using a pattern detection approach. The performances of the resulting maps of inter- row cover depends on the date of acquisition images. One way of improving this point maybe to use a combination of different sensors to maximise the image number acquired in the optimal period at the end of winter or at the beginning of spring. The number of trees can be quantified using a simple algorithm based on circle detection. Recent advances in Lidar technology will certainly provide new insights to improve the performances on this point. All of these remote sensing derived variables are useful to consider the spatial variability encountered at the watershed scale and can be integrate to better model the functioning of orchards and their management in future investigations. Despite this, ground observations will still be needed to validate the estimations and to obtain information on irrigation techniques at large scales. [SOURCE: Page_174]

2. Complementary ground measurements done on 5 orchards of the Ouvèze basin The selection of plots was done considering various soil and agricultural practices. Different information was analysed: surveys from farmers, soil map of the Vaucluse department provided by the Donesol database (INRAE). On these 5 fields, soil samples were analysed to assess water reserve easily available for the plant (RFU) according to the following equation: Available Reserve = Useful water * root depth - stone [SOURCE: Page_182]

Each plot was equipped by soil moisture sensors (capacitive probes) set up on the row and on the inter- row at three soil depth (5-15-30 cm). Fig S$_5$.3 shows an example of soil moisture measurements recorded on plot 3099 [SOURCE: Page_183]

For the largest field 3099, additional measurements have been done. Sap flows were analysed. Fig S$_5$.3b shows an example of such measurements in 2022. [SOURCE: Page_183]

Phenological observations and monitoring of leaf development. Annotations of tree phenology (according to the BBCH scale) were conducted to note the dates of the main stages, with a specific focus during the period between budburst and fruit growth stages (fig 5.2). Observations were done at the tree scale on two opposite branches, and for 3 trees per plot. 13 parcels in 2021, and 9 in 2022having different inter-row managements (with or without grass) were monitored with hemispherical photographs, taken once a month all along the year. Photos were taken both towards the ground aiming the surface in the interrow, and towards the sky aiming the tree canopy. All photos were processed from algorithms developed by UMR EMMAH based on the Can-eye software (CAN-EYE - Home (inrae.fr) to estimate biophysical variables. (see Lopez-Lozano R, Khaly Diop P, Courault D, Doussan C, Chanzy A, Debolini M, Rouault P, Flamain F, Pouget G, 2022. Monitoring phenology of cherry-tree orchards from remote sensing: analysis of fAPAR time-series to identify flowering and the start of fruit growth, International Phenology, 20-22 June Avignon, and https://gesoc^2022.sciencesconf.org/file/850257). 188 [SOURCE: Page_188]

Contextualisation et objectifs Ce chapitre porte sur l’évaluation du modèle SIMETAW pour quantifier les irrigations et l’évapotranspiration sur le bassin de l’Ouvèze-Ventoux. Ce modèle comprend un modèle de bilan hydrique avec une représentation simplifiée du sol et une estimation de l’EvapoTranspiration (ETa) calculée suivant une approche à double coefficients (un coefficient cultural défini pour chaque type de culture (Kc) et un coefficient de stress hydrique (Ks) déterminé en fonction de la quantité d’eau disponible dans le sol (SWC)). Il permet d’estimer des quantités d’eau à apporter par irrigation (QI) et la consommation en eau à l’échelle de la parcelle tout au long du cycle cultural pour différentes années climatiques. Le modèle, dans sa version standard, n’utilise pas de données de télédétection et distingue les parcelles uniquement par leur type de culture avec des coefficients culturaux qui ne varient pas entre les années ni entre les parcelles d’une même culture. La nouveauté apportée ici a été d’introduire des coefficients culturaux calculés à partir de variables dérivées de données Sentinel 2 (NDVI et FCOVER) estimées journalièrement pour chacune des parcelles du bassin. Une première analyse de sensibilité a été effectuée afin de sélectionner les paramètres les plus impactant sur QI, SWC et ETa. La seconde étape de ce travail a été de calibrer le modèle à partir des mesures effectuées sur le terrain. Les humidités des sols mesurées de façon continue depuis 2021 sur 5 parcelles du bassin ont été considérées. Des flux de sève mesurés sur une parcelle ont permis d’évaluer la simulation d’ETa sur les années 2022 et 2023. Les données sur les volumes d’eau utilisés pour l’irrigation fournies par l’ASA (gestionnaire d’eau) ainsi que des enquêtes réalisées auprès des exploitants ont permis d’évaluer les performances du modèle à simuler correctement les QI à la fois aux échelles parcellaires, sur 13 exploitations suivies sur plusieurs années et sur l’ensemble du bassin. Des comparaisons entre version initiale et version modifiée en utilisant les Kc dérivés de télédétection sont présentées et discutées par rapport à la précision obtenue en tenant compte des incertitudes des données utilisées. Le modèle donne des estimations des dynamiques d’eau dans le sol (SWC) proches des mesures des parcelles suivies (R²=0.7 et 0.9 pour les simulations de SWC en 2022 et 2023 respectivement). La simulation de la quantité d’eau utilisée pour l’irrigation par les différentes exploitations montre une bonne corrélation avec les données de l’ASA (R²= 0.81). L’application du modèle SIMETAW utilisant des Kc dérivés de la télédétection variant par parcelle montre une meilleure corrélation que la version standard sur QI, 3% d’écart avec la valeur mesurée par l’ASA contre 18 % avec la version où Kc ne varie pas. Ce travail a montré que l’apport de données Sentinel 2 améliore significativement les performances du modèle SIMETAW en prenant en compte la variabilité de développement des cultures à l’échelle du bassin versant. L’utilisation de ce modèle simplifié permet de simuler différents scénarii d’irrigation quantifiant les impacts sur la ressource en eau pour différentes pratiques. Ces informations sont particulièrement utiles pour aider les gestionnaires de l’eau à mieux prévoir les besoins futurs. 189 [SOURCE: Page_189]

Partie 1 - Résumé étendu 1. Introduction Ce chapitre inclut un article qui sera soumis prochainement à la revue Agriculture Water Management (ELSEVIER). Le bassin de l’Ouvèze-Ventoux doit réduire ses prélèvements d’eau pour l’agriculture de 30% d’ici 2030 suivant le plan de gestion des ressources en eau qui a été élaboré par l’agence de l’eau. Dans le contexte des changements climatiques, les épisodes de sécheresse et de canicules sont de plus en plus fréquents et augmentent les besoins en eau. Les ressources étant limitées, particulièrement sur l’Ouvèze qui doit maintenir un débit écologique minimale, des restrictions d’usages de l’eau pour irriguer sont de plus en plus appliquées même pour des pratiques telles que le goutte à goutte qui est considéré comme une technique parcimonieuse. Sur le bassin de l’Ouvèze-Ventoux les pratiques d’irrigation montrent une très grande variabilité en termes de fréquence et d’apports et il n’existe pas de cartes d’estimation des besoins en eau des cultures qui permettrait de distribuer l’eau de façon plus optimisée. Le modèle SIMETAW, qui simule des quantités d’eau à apporter à l’échelle de la parcelle en tenant compte de la consommation en eau réelle des cultures, a été choisi de répondre à ce point et évalué dans cette étude. Le modèle dans sa version standard ne permettait pas de différencier les parcelles d’une même culture suivant leur développement. L’objectif a été d’améliorer cette représentation de la variabilité spatiale et de comparer les performances obtenues à partir de différentes variables mesurées sur le bassin. Une fois le modèle bien calibré, différents scénarios de gestion de l’eau ou des inter-rangs peuvent être simulés pour quantifier les impacts sur les quantités d’eau d’irrigation nécessaire à l’échelle du bassin. Cette approche permet de fournir des indicateurs sur la ressource en eau en tenant compte de la variabilité des pratiques à l’échelle du bassin. [SOURCE: Page_190]

2. Matériel et méthodes 2.1 Le modèle SIMETAW et son adaptation pour l’appliquer sur le bassin de l’Ouvèze-Ventoux [SOURCE: Page_190]

Le modèle SIMETAW, initialement développé par une équipe de l’université de Californie (Snyder et al., 2012) pour estimer les besoins en eau d’irrigation de leur territoire, est basé sur un modèle simplifié de bilan hydrique à pas de temps journalier (1 seule couche de sol définie par sa profondeur et un coefficient de transfert qui dépend de la texture) couplé à une approche de type FAO à double coefficients pour estimer la consommation en eau des cultures (Eta). Le modèle simule une quantité d’eau dans le sol (SWC) tous les jours et des quantités d’eau qu’il faut apporter par irrigation pour subvenir au mieux aux besoins des cultures qui dépendent d’un seuil de stress défini en paramètre d’entrée et des caractéristiques techniques de l’irrigation. La valeur du coefficient cultural (Kc) rentrant dans le calcul de l’ETa est propre à chaque espèce cultivée et consiste en une valeur journalière définie en fonction de paramètres fixés en entrée. Ce choix de paramétrisation ne permet pas de prendre en compte les variabilités spatiales et temporelles de développement que l’on observe entre parcelles d’une même culture. Le modèle a ainsi été modifié de façon à utiliser des Kc calculés pour chaque parcelle en fonction des données Sentinel 2 (partie décrite très brièvement dans la section suivante et de façon plus détaillée dans l’article). [SOURCE: Page_190]

190 [SOURCE: Page_190]

2.2 Traitement des données satellitaires Les données Sentinel 2 ont été téléchargées depuis 2016 jusqu’à 2024 et l’indice de végétation NDVI et le FCOVER ont été calculés pour chaque parcelle à chaque date d’acquisition. Les valeurs sont ensuite interpolées à l’échelle de la journée (même procédé que décrit chapitre 5). Le calcul du Kc journalier est réalisé à partir de l’analyse de la relation linéaire observée entre FCOVER et NDVI pour chaque parcelle pour une année donnée, suivant l’approche proposée par Simonneaux et al., (2009). Le calcul fait intervenir une valeur de Kc maximale définie dans des tables fournies par Allen and Pereira, (2009) ; Kc max dépend du type de culture, de la densité d’arbres, ainsi que de son enherbement sur l’inter-rang. Ces caractéristiques sont dérivées des résultats des travaux présentés au chapitre 5. [SOURCE: Page_191]

2.3 Données de terrain servant pour la calibration et validation du modèle 2.3.1 Données mesurées [SOURCE: Page_191]

Cinq vergers du territoire sont équipés de sondes capacitives mesurant l’humidité du sol à différentes profondeur (de la surface à 50cm) sur le rang et l’inter-rang. Ces mesures fournissent une information toutes les 30 minutes qui ont été moyennées pour estimer le stock d’eau du sol à l’échelle de la journée et le comparer aux simulations journalières de stock d’eau dans le sol par SIMETAW (SWC). Sur la plus grande parcelle (3099), quatre arbres sont équipés de capteurs de flux de sève de type « SFM1x » installés en 2022 sur deux branches opposées par arbre. Les mesures à pas de temps horaire sont moyennées et fournissent le débit moyen journalier d’un arbre de la parcelle. Une valeur de transpiration a été obtenue à l’échelle de la parcelle en considérant la moyenne des valeurs obtenues sur les 4 arbres et en tenant compte du nombre de ramifications des arbres et du nombre d’arbres dans la parcelle (nombre d’arbres issu de la méthode présentée chapitre 5). La valeur finale est assimilée à une évapotranspiration est comparée aux simulations de l’ETa par SIMETAW. [SOURCE: Page_191]

2.3.2 Quantités d’eau apportée par irrigation [SOURCE: Page_191]

Différentes enquêtes ont été conduites permettant l’estimation de la consommation d’eau de 285 parcelles de 21 exploitations du territoire. Le calcul de la quantité d’eau apportée à l’année sur une parcelle prend en compte les caractéristiques techniques de l’irrigation et les pratiques de l’exploitant ou l’exploitante (voir chapitre 3) ainsi que l’espacement des arbres sur un rang et entre les rangs. L’ASA-OV, a fourni pour cette étude les données de quantité d’eau apportée sur l’ensemble bassin versant de 2006 à 2022 (quantités totales d’eau utilisée pour l’irrigation, QI) et QI sur 13 exploitations de 2006 à 2021. Ces données sont utilisées pour la validation des QI simulées par le modèle. [SOURCE: Page_191]

2.4 Analyses de sensibilité des paramètres de SIMETAW Dans un premier temps, une analyse de sensibilité des différents paramètres du modèle a été conduite afin de définir les paramètres principaux affectant les principales sorties du modèle à savoir : EvapoTranspiration réelle calculée (ETa), le coefficient de stress (Ks), le stock d’eau dans le sol (SWC) et [SOURCE: Page_191]

191 [SOURCE: Page_191]

les doses d’eau apportées sur l’année (QI) et par événement d’irrigation (MAD) ainsi que leur fréquence d’apport. Quatre paramètres ont ainsi été sélectionnés : Kc (issu ou non de télédétection), « Allowable Depletion » (AD) pouvant conduire à un un stress pour la plante qui entre dans le calcul du coefficient de stress Ks (valeur initiale 50% - variation entre 25 and 85%), le paramètre Theta qui correspond à un coefficient influençant le transfert d’eau dans le sol variant en fonction de la texture du sol (variation de 0.1 à 0.36) et le temps d’opération entrant dans le calcul de la dose d’eau apportée par évènement d’irrigation et définissant le nombre d’heures d’apport d’eau par évènement d’irrigation (variant de 1 à 20h par évènement). Après une meilleure compréhension de l’impact de ces paramètres sur les principales sorties d’intérêt, les valeurs de ces paramètres ont été optimisées pour les 5 parcelles sur lesquelles des mesures précises sont disponibles. Ces paramètres ont été définis tels que la simulation de SWC par le modèle soit le plus proche possible des dynamiques observées et que QI soit aussi proche des estimations des doses d’eau apportées calculées selon les enquêtes. [SOURCE: Page_192]

2.5 Simulation du volume d’eau d’irrigation par SIMETAW Différents scénarii ont été réalisés pour estimer la quantité d’eau totale apportée au bassin. D’abord l’utilisation de SIMETAW dans sa version « classique », i.e. sans apport de la télédétection (Kc considéré ici correspondant à celui proposé pour pêcher car pas de valeur pour cerisiers ou abricotiers) avec l’application pour toute la surface de vergers du paramétrage relatif à une parcelle étudiée de verger et l’application pour toute la surface de vignes de table de la simulation d’une parcelle de vigne (avec Kc relatif à la vigne de table fourni par SIMETAW). Cette méthode de simulation (Scenario S$_1$) est ici appliquée de trois façons différentes avec la paramétrisation de la parcelle de verger de plantation récente (peu d’apport d’eau – exemple rencontré sur la parcelle mesurée, 1824), une parcelle irriguée en goutte à goutte (apport d’eau moyen – parcelle 3099) et une parcelle avec irrigation par micro-asperseurs (plus fortement irriguée – parcelle 3347). Dans un second temps, les trois mêmes simulations du bassin ont été conduites mais cette fois avec un Kc calculé par télédétection pour trois parcelles de verger (avec également les trois variantes : jeune avec la parcelle 1824, goutte à goutte avec la parcelle 3099 ou micro- asperseur avec la parcelle 3347) et une de vigne de table avec la parcelle 53 (Scenario S$_2$). Enfin, une simulation sommant l’apport d’eau estimé de chaque parcelle de vergers et vignes de table du territoire par SIMETAW (Scenario S$_3$). Trois paramétrages différents (faible, moyenne ou forte irrigation des vergers) ont été simulés dans ce cas. Enfin, le scénario 4 correspond à une simulation où l’on applique les ratios des surfaces identifiées par la typologie des exploitations et parcelles (voir chapitre 3). [SOURCE: Page_192]

3. Résultats et discussions 3.1 Analyses de sensibilité et identification des paramètres clés L’analyse de sensibilité réalisée montre que le paramètre Theta affecte fortement la hauteur du stock d’eau dans le sol (SWC). Le Kc issu ou non de télédétection impacte, comme attendu, ETa et donc par un effet domino la quantité d’eau dans le sol. Le temps d’opération de l’irrigation, modifie fortement la dose d’eau apportée par évenement d’irrigation suivant les valeurs définies de 0.6 à 12mm et donc la [SOURCE: Page_192]

192 [SOURCE: Page_192]

fréquence des apports. Lorsque l’on augmente la dose apportée la quantité d’eau dans le sol et le coefficient de stress hydrique sont fortement modifiés comme attendu. [SOURCE: Page_193]

3.2 Calibration des parcelles suivies Les dynamiques de SWC simulées sont bien corrélées aux dynamiques observées dans les cinq parcelles suivies (R² de 0.7 et 0.9 respectivement pour les années 2022 et 2023). Une bonne corrélation est également observée entre les quantités d’irrigation (QI) annuelles modélisées et estimées d’après enquêtes pour les quatre parcelles irriguées suivies (entre 19 et 35mm d’écart selon les parcelles et les années). Il est à noter que l’utilisation d’un Kc issu de télédétection améliore la précision des estimations, et permet une meilleure prise en compte de la variabilité inter-parcellaire et surtout interannuelle en fonction des variations météorologiques et de développement réel du couvert. [SOURCE: Page_193]

4. Conclusion Le modèle SIMETAW a montré de bonnes performances après avoir été ajusté à partir de données collectées sur le terrain dans différents vergers irrigués. Cette étude présente la première utilisation du modèle SIMETAW pour des vergers de cerisiers, en se basant sur des données mesurées, des calendriers d'irrigation et des informations de consommation d’eau à large échelle. Les simulations sont nettement améliorées lorsque le Kc est estimé à partir de la télédétection, ce qui permet de mieux intégrer la variabilité spatiale du développement des cultures. Les résultats pour la consommation d'eau liée à l'irrigation à l'échelle du bassin versant sont satisfaisants, avec seulement 3 % de différence par rapport aux quantités d'eau réellement mesurées par les gestionnaires en 2021. La bonne précision de ce modèle est globalement satisfaisante pour estimer la consommation d’eau à l’échelle d’un bassin versant. L’estimation de la quantité d’eau par le modèle SIMETAW représente un outil utile aux gestionnaires d’eau qui peut leur permettre d’évaluer la quantité d’eau à distribuer à une large zone ou à des sous- zones de bassins pour différentes pratiques et différents scénarii climatiques. [SOURCE: Page_194]

194 [SOURCE: Page_194]

Partie 2 - Integration of Sentinel-2 data into SIMETAW model for assessing irrigation water requirements and evapotranspiration from the plot to a small Mediterranean watershed Pierre Rouault a, Dominique Courault a *, Fabrice Flamain a, Guillaume Pouget a, Claude Doussan a, Marcel El Hajj c, Samer K Al-Mashharawi c, Matthew McCabe c, Marta Debolini b [SOURCE: Page_195]

a UMR 1114 EMMAH INRAE, Avignon University, Agroparc, Domaine St Paul, route de l’aérodrome, 84914 Avignon, France b CMCC Foundation – Euro-Mediterranean Centre on Climate Change, IAFES Division, Sassari, Italy c King Abdullah University of Science and Technology, Thuwal, Makkah, Saudi Arabia * Corresponding author: Dominique Courault ([EMAIL_REDACTED]) [SOURCE: Page_195]

Abstract The water management for crop irrigation becomes a crucial point given the context of climate change and periods of increasing restriction in the summer months. Decision-support tools are needed to evaluate scenarios of various agricultural practices. The study aimed to assess the water requirements of orchards at the plot scale using the simplified water budget model, SIMETAW, and to apply this model at the scale of a small Mediterranean watershed to compare various water use scenarios. The standard version of SIMETAW integrates a water balance model which assumes a single uniform soil layer to calculate the crop water needs at the plot scale (QI). Evapotranspiration (ETa) is computed using a crop coefficient specific to each crop type (Kc) and a water stress coefficient (Ks) derived from the water available in the soil (SWC). In this standard version, plots are categorized by crop type, with fixed crop coefficients (Kc) that remain constant across years and between plots of the same crop type. To better capture the spatial variability of crops at watershed scale, SIMETAW was modified to incorporate remote sensed data. Crop coefficients for each crop in the basin were estimated using the relationship between NDVI and FCOVER (vegetation indice and cover fraction) derived from Sentinel-2 images. This modified SIMETAW version was compared to the standard version in estimating irrigation water requirements, from the field level to the watershed scale. An initial sensitivity analysis was carried out to select the parameters with the greatest impact on QI, SWC and ETa. The calibration of the model was carried out with field measurements. Soil moisture measured continuously since 2021 in 5 orchards in the watershed was considered. Sap flows measured on one orchard field were used to evaluate the crop transpiration for the years 2022 and 2023. Data on water volumes used for irrigation provided by the ASA (water manager at watershed scale) and surveys of farmers were used to assess the model's performance in [SOURCE: Page_195]

195 [SOURCE: Page_195]

simulating QI both at plot level, for 13 farms monitored over several years and at regional scale over the whole basin. Comparisons between the standard model version and the modified version using Kc from remote sensing are presented and discussed in relation to the accuracy obtained on water volumes, considering the uncertainties in the data used. The model gives estimates of Soil Water Content (SWC) close to the measurements of the monitored orchards (R²= 0.7 and 0.9 for the SWC simulation in 2022 and 2023 respectively). The simulation of the quantity of irrigation water in farms shows a good correlation with the ASA data (R²=0.81). SIMETAW has then been used to simulate different irrigation scenarios for assessing the impact of different practices on the water resources at the watershed scale. QI estimation showed a better performance when incorporating the remote sensed Kc (17% difference with the value measured from ASA) than Qi obtained with the standard version (66 % difference). The incorporation of Sentinel-2 data significantly enhanced the performance of the model by accounting for the variability in crop development across the catchment scale. By considering a typology of farms according to the irrigation practices (three classes were defined according to observations and survey, high, medium and low irrigation), the model was able to estimate water requirements with 10% difference with the ASA values. This information is particularly useful in helping water managers for decisions with regards to the evolution of water resources. Keywords: Modelisation, Irrigation, EvapoTranspiration, Orchard, Crop coefficient, Remote sensing [SOURCE: Page_196]

1. Introduction In the Mediterranean, there is an increasing water dependence of agriculture which leads to more competition between the various users at the territory scale. Almost 25 % of the arable land surface is irrigated (Azzopardi et al., 2020), but irrigation is facing more and more restrictions during the summer (Eekhout et al., 2024). Methods for providing the right quantity of water at the right time based on the estimation of the actual water requirements of crops, that consider the variability of techniques and environmental conditions, are still relevant and needed. Local sensors can monitor the soil water status and provide alerts for improved water management, but their coverage is limited (Santhiya and Radhakrishnan, 2024). Various modelling approaches to estimate crop water consumption have been proposed in the literature which can be classified according to the processes taken into account, the range of spatial and temporal scales and the variables used (Aloui et al., 2023). Lascano, (1991) proposed three categories of models related of the level of complexity represented: starting from the soil infiltration simulation, then the simulation of evaporation, and finally the approaches that consider soil- plant interaction. The mechanistic models detail the main water transfer processes and often require a lot of input parameters describing the system soil-plant. The semi-empirical models have a simplified representation of processes and generally involve less parameters to calibrate. There is therefore a [SOURCE: Page_196]

196 [SOURCE: Page_196]

compromise to be found between the most realistic representation of transfers, which often requires a large number of parameters to be calibrated and is therefore difficult to spatialise according to the data actually available. One of additional difficulties in simulating the water balance of orchards is also their heterogenous structure with inter-row and row that it makes the system to be modelled more complex. Among the mechanistic models simulating the soil water content, the Hydrus model (Upadhyaya et al., 2024) is one of the most well-known and has been applied not only on annual-crops but also on orchards (e.g. apple trees, Nazari et al., 2021). The introduction of cover in the inter-row impacts the cropping systems and water consumption. The WaLIS model has been developed to consider the water resource partitioning in such associations to evaluate the water regime in intercropped systems (Gaudin and Gary, 2012). There are many different approaches to estimating water fluxes in plots. Kool et al., (2014) has reviewed the available methods to separate evaporation and transpiration. The most commonly applied method is based on the use of a crop coefficient (Kc) defined for each crop type combined with the potential evapotranspiration provided from a weather station (Allen et al., 1998). Kc values can be found in different reports or regional catalogues (CABRL, 2019; Guerra et al., 2016). Kc calibrations have been carried out for a large number of regions, crop types, soil and climatic conditions, with a large body of results assessed against in situ measurements using lysimeters or values of evapotranspiration estimated from the Bowen ratio or eddy correlation techniques (Allen et al., 2005; Pereira et al., 2015). Whereas numerous papers have reported studies with Kc calibration on cereals, grasslands and vegetables, less literature exists about perennial crops. Measurements are more complex to acquire over heterogeneous land with large row and inter-rows, and when some values are proposed they are often very specific and do not differentiate species and/or varieties (Andreu-Coll et al., 2024; Guodong et al., 2016). The FAO56 method is easy to apply at regional scale because it requires very few inputs data, but also presents limits (Allen et al., 2005). Additional coefficients have been proposed to improve this point, as “The dual Kc approach”, which splits the Kc factor into a plant component Kcb and a soil component Ke. Ke requires generally more information on the soil properties and the soil moisture content. It can be estimated from different methods as described by (Allen et al., 2005; Rallo et al., 2021; Ramos et al., 2024). The evapotranspiration partitioning using this dual Kcc method has been tested for olives (Er-Raki et al., 2010; Rousseaux et al., 2009), vineyards (Ferreira et al., 2012), coffee (Flumignan et al., 2011), citrus (Er- Raki et al., 2009) and peach (Paço et al., 2012). Other models based on the resolution of the energy budget can also be used to estimate the latent heat flux (and thus evapotranspiration). They require radiative and surface temperature measurements, or eddy correlations fluxes. Vegetation can be represented as a single big leaf or described in terms of a multi-layer structure with or without separating the soil part from the canopy. The exchanges between soil, plant and air involve different resistance schemes, which are more and less complex to compute, 197 [SOURCE: Page_197]

depending on the data available (Derardja et al., 2024). Often such approaches use remote sensing data in the optical and thermal ranges, as surface temperature and albedo or net radiations can be derived from various satellites (Courault et al., 2005; Maes and Steppe, 2012). A large amount of research, and related literature, has explored such data to map evapotranspiration at various scales (Anderson et al., 2007; McCabe et al., 2019; Olioso et al., 2023; Yang et al., 2023). However, few applications have been carried on fruit trees (Ben-Gal et al., 2009; Cammalleri et al., 2010; Giuliani and Flore, 2000; Sepulcre- Cantó et al., 2007). A major constraint on the use of thermal data from operational satellites is their too low spatial and temporal resolutions (daily data at 1km or 100m every 16 days) and not suitable for monitoring at the field scale the efficiency of irrigation or for helping water management at farm scale. Future fine resolution missions in the thermal range will solve this issue, such as TRISHNA which will be launched in 2027 and will provide thermal data every 3 days at 50m (Roujean et al., 2021) or Earth daily satellites with launch planned in 2026 (https://earthdaily.com/constellation/) that will provide daily thermal data at 100m resolution. For the management of irrigation, crop models are often used such as STICS (Brisson et al., 2003), DSSAT (Jones et al., 2003), Optirrig (Richard et al., 2022) or Aquacrop (Rallo et al., 2012). All these models need calibration from ground observations. If large crop database already exist for a wide variety of annuals (Brisson et al., 2004), very few exist for perennial. Alternative methods requiring less calibration parameters have been proposed for irrigation monitoring, among which the SIMETAW model initially developed by the California University at Davis to assess the future agricultural water demand at the state level (Snyder et al., 2012). The main features of this model are the simulation of daily evapotranspiration, from a dual coefficient approach, and the computation of an irrigation schedule for each field and year. A simplified water balance is computed at a daily time step with a minimal set of soil information (soil depth and maximum water holding capacity) and of irrigation parameters (start and end period, duration, and flow). This model was integrated into a GIS spatial platform under R programming language to be applied at a large scale over the whole Europe for analysing the impact of climatic change on water requirements by Masia et al., (2021) or at the USA scale for comparisons of evapotranspiration (Kibria et al., 2021). It has been also used at a finer spatial scale for 3 different sites in California with peach and orange trees, and wheat (Mancosu et al., 2016) giving satisfactory results compared to ground measurements. The main advantage of this modelling approach is that it computes both evapotranspiration and irrigation quantities from very few input data. One limitation is that there is only a single dataset of parameters for each crop type. Kc varies according to the season for each crop, but the model does not consider the spatial variability for a same crop type between the different fields of a watershed which can be due to the exposition or variety or management by the farmers. Against this backdrop, different scientific questions arise: 1) is it possible to use Sentinel 2 data to assess crop coefficients of various Mediterranean orchards and include them in the SIMETAW model to better 198 [SOURCE: Page_198]

estimate the water requirements of orchards at the watershed scale? 2) How does accounting for the spatial variability of plots improve upon the standard version? 3) What is the impact of various irrigation strategies on the total water quantities used at the watershed scale? The aim of this study was to reply to these questions assessing SIMETAW performances on a small Mediterranean watershed where most of the agricultural land use is represented by irrigated orchards and vineyards. The paper is structured as follows: a first part describes the main assumptions and equations of the SIMETAW model. Then the database acquired on the case study is presented as well as the approach developed to introduce remote sensing data into the model. The results are displayed in section 3, that further discussed in the last section. [SOURCE: Page_199]

2. Methodology 2.1          SIMETAW model The SIMETAW model is a crop-soil-water balance model which computes daily crop evapotranspiration (ETa), irrigation requirements (QI) and schedules from a dual coefficient approach (Kc for the crop coefficient and Ks a stress coefficient computed from the water balance) (figure 6.1). The estimated volume of irrigation is the water needed to refill the water losses from the root zone due to the evapotranspiration. Different papers describe the different parts of the model in Mancosu et al., (2016), Masia et al., (2021) and Snyder et al., (2012). In the following sections, the main equations and assumptions of the SIMETAW model version are briefly presented. This version is the SIMETAW_R version used in particular by Masia et al., (2021). A standard dataset of crop parameters was supplied at the same time as the original SIMETAW_R script. [SOURCE: Page_199]

199 [SOURCE: Page_199]

Figure 6.1. General description of the main functional packages of the SIMETAW model (boxes in blue are related to the water balance and boxes in green are related to the crop characteristics) [SOURCE: Page_200]

Daily Soil Water Depletion (SWDday) is assessed as: 𝑆𝑊𝐷𝑑𝑎𝑦 = 𝐹𝐶 − 𝑆𝑊𝐶𝑑𝑎𝑦                                                                              eq. 6.1 With 𝐹𝐶 = 2 × 𝑃𝐴𝑊 where FC is the soil field capacity and PAW, the Plant Available Water defined in equation 6.9 depending on the root depth (which is fixed as a parameter of the model). The equation used to compute FC is a simplified approach proposed by Snyder et al., (2012). SWCday is the soil water content of the current day estimated from the water balance model. The basic equation considers the Irrigation (Ir), Rainfall (R) and actual evapotranspiration (ETa), all in mm. 𝑆𝑊𝐶𝑑𝑎𝑦 = 𝑆𝑊𝐶𝑑𝑎𝑦−1 − 𝐸𝑇𝑎𝑑𝑎𝑦 + 𝑅𝑑𝑎𝑦 + 𝐼𝑟𝑑𝑎𝑦                                                         eq. 6.2 Actual evapotranspiration (ETa) is the product of the potential Evapotranspiration (ET$_0$), computed from the Penman Monteith equation from standard climatic data (Penman, 1948) multiplied by a crop coefficient Kc and a stress coefficient Ks (detailed in eq. 6.8). 𝐸𝑇𝑐 = 𝐸𝑇0 × 𝐾𝑐 and 𝐸𝑇𝑎 = 𝐸𝑇𝑐 × 𝐾𝑠                                                                 eq. 6.3 [SOURCE: Page_200]

where ETc is the daily maximum evapotranspiration for a given crop [SOURCE: Page_200]

2.1.1 Crop coefficient (Kc) estimation In the standard version proposed by Snyder et al., (2012), the determination of Kc slightly differs from the FAO method (Allen et al., 1998), since 9 parameters are required to define the Kc pattern per crop [SOURCE: Page_200]

200 [SOURCE: Page_200]

(see Table 6.1 and figure 6.2a). 5 periods (A to E) are defined. The different dates separating these periods from the sowing to the end of the vegetative cycle or the harvest date must be provided as input data for each crop, and the percentage of the length of each period of the cycle (growth maturity, senescence) with the corresponding Kc values (see figure 6.2a and Table 6.1). Kc_B is applying at the date B and Kc_D is applying at the date E. Before the date B and after the date E, a null coefficient is applying because the assumption is to consider that there is no cover at these periods. The season begins with rapid growth from Date B. The Kc_C fixed in the table 6.1 is used to calculate a Kc midseason according to Allen approach (Allen et al, 2005) which is applied for all the third period (period C in figure 6.2a). During the second and the fourth period (B and D) of irrigation, a linear relationship is calculated between the Kc_B and the Kc midseason and between the Kc midseason and the Kc_D. The Kc midseason is calculated with a climate correction as follows: Kcmidseason = 𝐾𝑐𝐶 + 0.1654 × (𝐸𝑇0𝑚𝑖𝑑𝑠𝑒𝑎𝑠𝑜𝑛 − 7.3 ) × (𝐾𝑐𝐶 − 1)                                       eq.6.4 Where ET0_midseason is the midseason average value of daily ET$_0$ during the C period and Kc_C the fixed value of Kc for the C period which depends on the crop type. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Figure 6.2. a) Scheme of the Kc estimation with the different required parameters for each crop type (5 periods A, B , C, D and E are delimited by dates defined in table 1, each period is defined by its percentage of duration in the season (for this example, inflection points in the Kc curve occur at 20% and 50% and 80%); b) examples of Kc graphs prescribed for different orchards in the standard version provided with the SIMETAW_R script (the input values for the inflexion points and duration percentages of two classes (plum and peach) are given in table 1) In the standard version, parameters for 18 perennial classes were defined, among them: almonds, apples, kiwifruits, stone fruits, walnuts, peaches, plums, avocados, grapefruits, lemons, dates, evergreens cultures, olives, oranges and small oranges and vineyards (table and wine grapes), see examples in figure 2b. No data were provided for cherry or apricot trees. The methodology proposed to defined Kc of the orchards in this study area is described in section 2.2. [SOURCE: Page_201]

201 [SOURCE: Page_201]

Table 6.1. Input parameters used to compute Kc for each crop type and values for plum and peach trees given in the standard version of SIMETAW Parameter   Variability for 18   Plum trees     Peach tree Category of required parameters name      crop classes planting date/or sowing (date A)            A            [01/01 - 01/05]      March 1st     March 1st Date of harvest/ or end of leaf (date E)    E            [18/08 – 30/12]     October 15th   August 15th Perc_B            [0-0]               0             0 period duration in percentage of Kc (%)     Perc_C           [22-50]             50             50 Perc_D           [67-90]             90             90 KcB             [0.2-1.15]          0.55           0.45 KcC             [0.55-1.2]          1.15           0.85 Kc values for the specific period KcD             [0.55-1.2]          1.15           0.85 KcE             [0.4-1.15]          0.65           0.6 [SOURCE: Page_202]

There is the possibility to consider an additional ground cover for a given crop, which can represent grass in the inter-row. This modification was proposed in the standard model according to the Allen and Pereira, (2009) approach. It consists of adding a Kc value each day, representing the presence of grass in the crop (see equation 5). The total KcT (crop + grass) is bounded at a maximum of 1.22. [SOURCE: Page_202]

𝐾𝑐𝑇𝑑𝑎𝑦 = 𝐼𝐾𝑐𝑑𝑎𝑦 + 𝐾𝑐𝐺𝐶                                                                                eq. 6.5 [SOURCE: Page_202]

With KcT: crop coefficient of the heterogeneous crop, IKc: Kc for the main crop computed from the equation 6.4 with values defined in the table 6.1 according to the crop type and KcGC: Kc for the second ground cover defined (e.g grass for orchard). The Kc value before the planting date (A) and after the harvesting date (E) is fixed as 0, which assumes that there is no evapotranspiration. It does not accurately represent the reality. To correct this point, a Ked coefficient has been added in the standard model to consider the evaporation of bare soil. A first step consists in using the formulation proposed by Ventura et al., (2006) described as follows in equation 6.6. [SOURCE: Page_202]

𝐾𝑒𝑑 = 2.54 ×  𝐸𝑇0𝑑 −0.5                                                                              eq. 6.6 [SOURCE: Page_202]

Where ΣET$_0$ is the cumulative ET$_0$ from the beginning of the cultural year up to the given day (d). A second step computes a second KE$_2$ coefficient according to equation 7 (Allen et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_202]

𝐾𝐸2 = 1.22 − 0.04 × 𝐸𝑇0                                                                               eq.6.7 [SOURCE: Page_202]

The final Ke is the lowest value between KE$_2$ and Ked. [SOURCE: Page_202]

202 [SOURCE: Page_202]

2.1.2 Stress coefficient (Ks) estimation The stress coefficient (Ks) depends on the soil water depletion (SWD) computed from the water balance and two parameters defined in the input data: Plant Available Water (PAW) which is computed from the maximum root depth (mm) and a textural parameter Theta (Orang et al., 2004) and the allowable depletion (AD expressed in %). AD represents the amount of available water corresponding to the yield threshold depletion (the default value in the model is set at 50%). [SOURCE: Page_203]

SWDday 100×(       )−AD PAWday Ksday = 1 −        100−AD eq. 6.8 [SOURCE: Page_203]

𝑃𝐴𝑊 = 𝑅𝑜𝑜𝑡. 𝑑𝑒𝑝𝑡ℎ × 𝑇ℎ𝑒𝑡𝑎                                                                            eq. 6.9 [SOURCE: Page_203]

Theta is fixed at 0.175mm per mm in Masia et al., (2021) and in Swelam, (2012). Orang et al. (2004) defined Theta value from the soil texture and proposed 3 values: 0.075 for “light soils”, 0.125 for “medium soils” and 0.175 for “heavy soils”. According to equation 6.1, the Theta value can be estimated from FC value and root depth. In numerous papers, FC can also be estimated from pedotransfer functions from the soil texture (see for example Mahfouf and Noilhan, (1996)). [SOURCE: Page_203]

2.1.3 Estimation of irrigation practices The irrigation dose, indicated as MAD or Management of Allowable Depletion, is calculated according to the irrigation characteristics (Table 6.2). Three techniques are possible: drip, sprinkler or flooding. For each irrigation type, six parameters are involved in the following equation and described in Table 6.2. [SOURCE: Page_203]

WAPF          𝐺𝑟 𝑀𝐴𝐷 = Pfap × (1 − 100) × 𝑂𝑡 × 𝐴𝑅 × 𝐷𝑢                                                       eq. 6.10 [SOURCE: Page_203]

Where: -   MAD is the water amount which is possible to apply to refill the soil to the field capacity for a day (in mm), -   Ot is the runtime per irrigation event. It depends on the irrigation technique (for example for drip irrigation, it can be 1h per day in summer), -   AR is the application rate (i.e. the flow of the used devices expressed in mm/h), -   Du is the distribution uniformity of irrigation (in m²/m²); -   WAPF is the percentage of water allocation and -   Pfap the percentage of full plant area for fully irrigated crops, WAPF and Pfap are most of the time set at 100%. [SOURCE: Page_203]

203 [SOURCE: Page_203]

Table 6.2. Standard parameters proposed in the irrigation computation by the standard SIMETAW Model and typical values given for two modes: M: micro-sprinkler and D: drip Irrigation parameter             Order of magnitude for drip/sprinkler [SOURCE: Page_204]

Ot      runtime (number of hours of irrigation)        D:24h/event; M:24h/event AR         Application rate (flow in mm/h)                     D:1.3; M:0.7 Du        Distribution uniformity (in m²/m²)                  D:0.85; M:0.8 Gr                  Gravity runoff                    0 (not used in this study case) WAPF    Water allocation as percentage of full                    100% Pfap         Percentage of full plant area                        100% [SOURCE: Page_204]

The model simulates an irrigation schedule in terms of Net Application Dose (NAD), i.e. the application of the MAD dose which is defined according to the SWD of the given day. If the depletion is higher than the possible MAD dose, the dose is applied. The date of irrigation depends on the soil water content computed from the water balance and the threshold value fixed for AD (Mancosu et al., 2016). The annual sum of each applied MAD corresponds to the total volume of annual irrigation of the field (QI). The schedule of irrigation days or not was named NAC (Net Application Content). The number of irrigations (Ni) per year is also computed. The equation 6.11 shows the relationship between Annual irrigation, Ni and MAD. [SOURCE: Page_204]

𝑄𝐼 (𝐴𝑛𝑛𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝑖𝑟𝑟𝑖𝑔𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛) = 𝑀𝐴𝐷 × 𝑁𝑖                                                                      eq. 6.11 [SOURCE: Page_204]

In addition to the input data described in Tables 6.1 (for Kc computation) and 6.2 (for the estimation of irrigation volumes), the other data necessary to run the model are detailed in Table 6.3. [SOURCE: Page_204]

2.2          Ouvèze-Ventoux watershed and available data SIMETAW was applied to a small Mediterranean watershed (99km²) in southeast France, the Ouvèze- Ventoux watershed (center 44°13N - 5°14E). The Ouvèze river flowing into the Rhône is the main water resource for irrigation in the area. The techniques are either micro-sprinkler or using pipes with drippers (Roux et al., 2019). Most crops are located on a sandy to loamy plateau ranging from 205m to 639m above sea level (a.s.l). The main crops are vineyards, representing 46% of the area, with 323ha wine vineyard and 65ha of table vineyard according to LPIS 202034, and orchards (31%) among which the most represented are cherry trees (Prunus avium), covered by a protected designation of origin (PDO) "Cerises du Mont-Ventoux" (Turpin, 2022) (figure 3). Since vineyards are little irrigated (<150mm/year) or not at all, (particularly wine vines, in order to comply with regulations), orchards are the crops that require the most water in this area (300mm in average). Other crops in the area include truffle trees (often little irrigated <20mm/year) and annual crops with little or no irrigation (meadows, cereals, very small areas of market gardening, etc.). [SOURCE: Page_205]

34 https://eurodatacube.com/marketplace/data-products/lpis [SOURCE: Page_205]

Figure 6.3. a) Map of landforms in the Ouvèze-Ventoux watershed with the main crops, b) sap flows on two branches on a cherry tree, c) photos of the diversity of practices (non-grassed and grassed orchards and vineyard), d) drip irrigation on a table vineyard, e) drip irrigation from two pipes on a cherry tree orchard. The climate, as recorded by the Carpentras weather station which is located 15 km SSO, typically experiences annual rainfall of 650mm, predominantly during the autumn-winter period and annual temperature of 14.5°C, with hot summer. Table S1 (given in supplementary material) summarises the characteristics of the three last studied years, 2021 was slightly more wet than the two recent years warmer and drier. In 2022, there has been significant meteorological drought, leading to irrigation restrictions. The distribution of water for irrigation is managed by the Association Syndicale Autorisée of the Ouvèze- Ventoux (ASA OV) over 1354 hectares with pressurized water pipes linking a network of terminals. Each farmer has one or more terminals distributing water to one or more fields. Water consumption is then invoiced each year by the ASA-OV on a pro rata basis. The ASA provided us yearly values of water consumption for 13 farmers from 2018 to 2021 and for the whole watershed from 2018 to 2022. These data have been used to validate the outputs of SIMETAW. However, the ASA equipment is not renovated and high water losses in the network are usual and not regularly monitored by farmers. For these reasons, the accuracy of this water consumption data is estimated with a margin of error around 30%. To complete this dataset, accurate surveys have been performed beside several farmers, selected to cover the variability of the watershed in terms of crops, soils, and water management. Twenty-one farmers have been surveyed about their agricultural practices to get accurate data at field scale on: start and end of irrigation, dose and technique used, crop cultivar, harvest dates and different 206 [SOURCE: Page_206]

labour or pruning/cuts done. These observations were compiled for 648 parcels, among them 359 orchards. The analysis of the variability of the agricultural practices is described in Rouault et al., (2024a, 2024b). From these data, the yearly sum of the net application irrigation (NAD) have been computed for each field as follows: [SOURCE: Page_207]

𝐿 𝑁𝐴𝐷𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑝 = ( 𝐿𝑡𝑜𝑡 × 𝑄𝑑 × 𝑡)⁄𝑆𝑢𝑟𝑓                                                                 eq. 6.12 𝑑 [SOURCE: Page_207]

𝑁𝐴𝐷𝑚𝑖𝑐𝑟𝑜𝑠𝑝𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑘𝑙𝑒𝑟 = (𝑇𝑑 × 𝑁𝑠 × 𝑆𝑢𝑟𝑓 × 𝐹𝑙𝑜𝑤 × 𝑡)⁄𝑆𝑢𝑟𝑓                                            eq. 6.13 [SOURCE: Page_207]

Where, Ltot: total pipe length, Ld: space between 2 drippers, t: irrigation duration per year, Surf: field surface, Qd: dripper flow, Td: tree density, Ns: number of micro-sprinklers per tree, Flow: sprinkler flow. [SOURCE: Page_207]

The row number is computed for 1 ha from the inter-row distance, then the pipe length is derived per field according to the field size and the row number and considering 1m of border in each size of the 1ha plot. The figure S6.1a in supplementary material shows the irrigation doses per irrigation event values obtained for the different farms and figure S6.1b the Ot distributions, i.e. the number of hours per irrigation event, according to surveys. Generally, for drip irrigation, the common practice is to bring water every day or every two days (around 2 or 3mm/day) between April and October. However, these surveys showed a large variability of drip irrigation practices. Some farms use very little water (minimum calculated 30mm/year), while others irrigate heavily more (maximum calculated around 750mm/year). There is a diversity of irrigation techniques: some farmers irrigate by drippers only 1 hour each day (from one or two pipes per row), others more than 20 hours but only once or twice a month. Table 4 shows the values defined for the irrigation parameters of SIMETAW derived from surveys. The most common practice (observed) is an irrigation every day during 2 hours. So, the value of Ot has been fixed for this practice at 2. For micro-sprinkler irrigation, there is also a great variability of practices, some plots are irrigated up to 415mm/year and other received 550mm/year, the irrigation time can vary from 2 to 3 hours. The Ot value has been set at 2.5 hours per event for this practice. For the Du parameter, the same value proposed by Masia et al., (2021) was used, i.e 85 %, because the irrigation located on the row with drip irrigation and between the trees for micro-sprinkler finally covers a large part of the field. For the other parameters such as the Water Allocation as Percentage of full (WAPF) and Pfap, there were set at 100% considering that these parameters referring to the root distribution in the soil and that the roots cover the whole plot. Due to the lower dose applied to young orchards according to surveys and the existing literature (Franco et al., 2000; Iglesias et al., 2024; Maes and Steppe, 2012; Pérez-López et al., 2007), different parameters were defined for this case. The Ot value was decreased by considering 1-hour irrigation (Ot=1). The identification of young orchards at the watershed scale is possible from a threshold applied to the NDVI using Sentinel 2 images as shown in Rouault et al., (2024b). As these orchards are always drip-irrigated 207 [SOURCE: Page_207]

according to surveys, this field characteristic leads to an irrigation dose (MAD) of 0.6mm/event. Table 6.4. Irrigation parameters fixed from farm’s surveys and MAD dose calculated from this value (eq.6.10) Drip                  Micro-sprinkler Ot          2h/event (1h for young orchard)      2.5h/event Ar                        0.7                       1.3 Du                       0.85                       0.8 WAPF                      100                       100 Pfap                      100                       100 MAD      1.2mm/event (0.6mm/event for young)   2.6mm/event [SOURCE: Page_208]

The 21 surveyed farms cover around 20% of the area. For the remaining plots, the farm classification resulting from a deeper analysis of the spatialized databases (LPIS, DEM35, soil and landuse^36 maps) done by Rouault et al., (2024a) was used. Two groups of farms have been identified, the farms which use a large amounts of irrigation water (average value above 500mm per year corresponding to farms having mainly irrigated orchards or table vineyards), and farms which consume much less (<100mm/year), characterized by high proportion of non-irrigated fields, or with cereals and wine grape vineyards. In the heavily irrigated farms, some orchards plots are defined as mediumly irrigated and others as heavily irrigated. This classification considers the diversity of practices according to the plot characteristics (method described in Rouault et al., 2024a). The ratio between these 3 orchard’s categories is 20.8% of heavily irrigated plots, 38.4% of medium irrigated plots and 40.8% of lowly irrigated plots. [SOURCE: Page_208]

2.2.1 Ground measurements More accurate data were also available from ground measurements for some fields and used for validation at field scale. A micro-meteorological station was installed in May 2022 in the centre of the largest cherry orchard (4.5ha) of the watershed to measure relative humidity, air temperature, wind speed in the two horizonal directions, net radiation, rainfall, surface temperature, soil and heat fluxes at a 15 minutes time step (see in supplementary material Part 1 the description of the sensors figure S.6.16 and table S$_4$). For previous years, climatic data were taken from the weather station of Carpentras^37 (located 15 km SSO of the study area). Daily potential evapotranspiration was computed from the Penman Monteith formula (Penman, 1948) and used directly as input data in the SIMETAW model. Soil moisture was measured in five different orchards, with capacitive probes arranged at varying depths between 0 to 50 cm (0-15, 15-30, 30-50cm). Continuous data were averaged every 30min from 15 May 2021 up to now, on both the row and the inter-row. The five fields were selected to cover variability encountered at the watershed scale in terms of soil, irrigation practice and inter-row management (Table 5). Additionally, measurements of water retention in the soil was conducted and values for Field Capacity (FC) and Permanent Wilting Point (PWP) were obtained at respectively 0.3 bar and 15 bars. These values allow to estimate the available water reserve (AWR) for 1m depth soil. AWR varied between 129mm to 162mm. The AD value was modified at 10% against 50% generally for the non-irrigated orchard, this value was more relevant according to the acceptable dynamic simulated of SWC. The table 6.5 reports also to the values of irrigation parameters used for the SIMETAW calibration for these 5 orchards fields. [SOURCE: Page_209]

For the largest field (identifier 3099), trees’ water-uptake rates are monitored using sap flows (SFM1x- ICT International) located at four different points of the field (Al-Mashharawi et al., 2024). The four locations have not started at the same periods. At each location, two sensors are installed on two branches of one tree. It was assumed that the measured water-uptake rate of the selected trees was similar to the majority of trees within that field. The collected water uptake data were processed 10- minute intervals using the Sap Flow Tool software (ICT International Pty. Ltd). The total flow rate of each of the four trees was determined by taking the average of the two SFMs in a tree and multiplying it by the number of branches. Finally, the average flow rate of the four trees was multiplied by the number of trees in the field obtained from ground observations (Rouault et al., 2024b) and divided by the area of the field. The figure 6.4 shows the different measurements of the field 3099 for the year 2022. Even if drip irrigation brings water every day in summer (2h/day – 1.5mm), this water amount is not enough to maintain the water stock at a high level. The climatic demand is high (ET$_0$ >5mm/day particularly in July and August up to 7.5mm/d) and the transpiration derived from the sap flow average is around 3.5mm/day. The stock of water available in the soil, integrated over a soil depth of 50 cm, decreases in summer but does not reach the level of the permanent wilting point (PWP) of the plots which, for this example, is 51mm for the plot illustrated. [SOURCE: Page_210]

210 [SOURCE: Page_210]

Figure 6.4. Ground measurements on the largest cherry tree field (3099) in 2022 a) ET$_0$ (mm/day), b) water uptake from sap flows (mm/day), water stock (mm/day) and d) daily rainfall (mm/day). The two vertical red lines show the irrigation period [SOURCE: Page_211]
